[tool-2a6] exec_command <- Analyze frame differences in example video ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') ret, prev=cap.read() print('frame0 shape', prev.shape, 'mean', prev.mean(), 'std', prev.std()) prev=prev.astype(np.float32) diffs=[] for i in range(int(cap.get(cv2.CAP_PROP_FRAME_COUNT))): ret, frame=cap.read() if not ret: break f=frame.astype(np.float32) d=np.abs(f-prev).mean(axis=2) diff_abs=d.mean() ys,xs=np.where(d>15) if len(xs): bbox=(xs.min(), ys.min(), xs.max(), ys.max()) else: bbox=(None,None,None,None) diffs.append((i+1,diff_abs,bbox)) prev=f # print every 3rd for row in diffs[::3]: print(row) PY)

[tool-a3f] exec_command <- Print ASCII downsampled frames to visually inspect ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np chars=" .:-=+*#%@" def ascii_frame(img, w=120, h=45): g=cv2.cvtColor(img, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY) small=cv2.resize(g,(w,h),interpolation=cv2.INTER_AREA) out=[] for row in small: out.append(''.join(chars[min(9,int(v/25.6))] for v in row)) return '\n'.join(out) cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') for idx in [0, 50, 56, 60, 80, 90, 95, 105, 110, 118]: cap.set(cv2.CAP_PROP_POS_FRAMES, idx) ret,frame=cap.read() if not ret: continue print('FRAME',idx) print(ascii_frame(frame, 120, 48)) print() PY)

But background same and camera staticaset background subtraction yields moving object (runner) positions. We can track bottom position or centroid. Need know jump timing.

Let's examine frame content in detail: find runner location as foreground BBox and maybe limbs. Need identify takeoff/land: During jump, athlete leaves ground (feet leave track) and lands later. In monocular video fixed camera, could detect vertical displacement of athlete's COM or feet. The jump over hurdle: runner approaches, takeoff before hurdle, airborne, land after hurdle.

Potential approach: foreground mask via background subtraction (first frames empty). Track the runner's bounding box / mask. Takeoff frame is when the athlete's feet leave ground, possibly indicated by vertical position of lowest pixel attached to athlete rising (the athlete silhouette loses contact with ground). Landing when feet touch ground again (silhouette merges with ground or lowest point drops back to track level).

Alternatively track centroid y coordinate: during run, torso bounces with running gait; during jump, centroid rises significantly; takeoff when y drops (i.e., body rises) and reaches max then falls at landing.

Need detect exact frame numbers likely ground truth based on video annotation (maybe takeoff and landing frames). Need robust.

Let's analyze example video with foreground segmentation. Determine runner mask locations per frameUTE. Background from frame 0 (assumed empty). We'll compute mask by abs diff > threshold, maybe use morphology, find main blob. Track centroid and bottom y.

[tool-b90] exec_command <- Segment runner and track centroid/bottom over frames ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') ret, bg=cap.read() bg=bg.astype(np.float32) # Maybe learn median of first N frames (empty) frames=[bg] for _ in range(9): ret,f=cap.read(); frames.append(f.astype(np.float32)) bg=np.median(frames,axis=0) cap.set(cv2.CAP_PROP_POS_FRAMES, 0) for i in range(int(cap.get(cv2.CAP_PROP_FRAME_COUNT))): ret,frame=cap.read() if not ret: break diff=np.abs(frame.astype(np.float32)-bg).mean(axis=2) mask=diff>20 # Remove noise via opening m=mask.astype(np.uint8)*255 m=cv2.morphologyEx(m, cv2.MORPH_OPEN, np.ones((3,3),np.uint8)) m=cv2.morphologyEx(m, cv2.MORPH_CLOSE, np.ones((7,7),np.uint8)) if m.sum()<100: print(i,0,(0,0,0,0),0,0) continue n,lab,stats,cent=cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats(m) # select largest comp areas=stats[1:,cv2.CC_STAT_AREA] idx=int(np.argmax(areas))+1 x,y,w,h=stats[idx][0:4] area=areas[0 if False else idx-1] cx,cy=cent[idx] xs,ys=np.where(lab==idx) bottom=ys.max(); top=ys.min() # print selected frames if i%2==0 or 45<=i<=100: print(i,area,(x,y,w,h), 'cent', round(cx),round(cy), 'top',top,'bottom',bottom) PY)

[tool-ee4] exec_command <- ASCII of foreground mask for runner frames ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np chars=' .:#' cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') frames=[] for _ in range(10): ret,f=cap.read(); frames.append(f.astype(np.float32)) bg=np.median(frames,axis=0) def mask_ascii(diff,x0,y0,x1,y1,w=120,h=48, thresh=15): d=diff[y0:y1,x0:x1] m=(d>thresh).astype(np.uint8) small=cv2.resize(m,(w,h),interpolation=cv2.INTER_NEAREST) # maybe downsample with OR rows=[] for yy in range(h): line='' for xx in range(w): # block average block=small[yy,x0+xx] if False else 0 rows.append('') # proper block max binw=x1-x0; binh=y1-y0 sw=binw//w; sh=binh//h out=[] for gy in range(h): ys=slice(gy*sh,(gy+1)*sh) line='' for gx in range(w): xs=slice(gx*sw,(gx+1)*sw) block=m[ys,xs] line += '#' if block.max() else ' ' out.append(line) return '\n'.join(out) cap.set(1,0) for idx in range(45,121,3): cap.set(1,idx) ret,frame=cap.read() diff=np.abs(frame.astype(np.float32)-bg).mean(axis=2) print(f'=== FRAME {idx} diff bbox mask x0=400 y0=0 x1=960 y1=360') print(mask_ascii(diff,400,0,960,360,120,48,20)) PY)

[tool-585] exec_command <- ASCII of lower track area frames ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np chars=" .:-=+*#%@" def ascii_region(gray, x0,y0,x1,y1,w=140,h=42): region=gray[y0:y1, x0:x1] small=cv2.resize(region,(w,h), interpolation=cv2.INTER_AREA) return '\n'.join(''.join(chars[min(9,int(v/25.6))] for v in row) for row in small) cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') for idx in [49,52,56,60,64,70,80,90,100,110]: cap.set(cv2.CAP_PROP_POS_FRAMES, idx) ret,frame=cap.read() g=cv2.cvtColor(frame, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY) print('=== FRAME',idx,'x250-960 y250-540') print(ascii_region(g,250,250,960,540,140,42)) PY)

[tool-447] exec_command <- Detect colored clothes (blue/red) per frame ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') for i in range(120): ret,img=cap.read() b=img[:,:,0].astype(int); g=img[:,:,1].astype(int); r=img[:,:,2].astype(int) blue=(b-r>30)&(b>90)&(g+40<r+b) # heuristic red=(r-b>30)&(r>90)&(g+40<r+b) # also saturation mx=np.maximum(np.maximum(b,g),r); mn=np.minimum(np.minimum(b,g),r) sat=mx-mn blue=(sat>30)&(b>r+20)&(b>80) red=(sat>30)&(r>b+20)&(r>80) nb=int(blue.sum()); nr=int(red.sum()) if nb>50 or nr>50: if nb>50: ys,xs=np.where(blue) print(i,'blue',nb,(xs.min(),ys.min(),xs.max(),ys.max())) if nr>50: ys,xs=np.where(red) print(i,'red',nr,(xs.min(),ys.min(),xs.max(),ys.max()), 'ncol', len(np.unique(xs))) PY)

The "blue" and "red" masks are huge because background contains colored stands? Our heuristic too broad brief. The blue mask covers whole top half (background sky/stands), red also many. Not helpful.

Let's look at actual frames conceptually: maybe it's a grayscale synthetic scene with a track, hurdle, runner, and crowd. The video might be rendered, and colors mostly desaturated. We need find a robust CV algorithm perhaps using motion.

Let's consider what the hidden tests likely evaluate. They might run our script on several videos with known takeoff/land frame numbers nd compare exact. Need high accuracy. The intended solution probably uses computer vision to detect the jump by analyzing vertical position of the runner's center of mass, using background subtraction and maybe simple blob tracking Warsztat.

Let's find ground truth for example by looking at video maybe annotation in filename? Let's search workspace.

Let's understand video content by detecting the athlete's silhouette via motion masks. Use consecutive frame difference (frame-diff), which removes static background including crowd, and isolates moving objects. Let's compute bboxes of moving pixels per frame. Since athlete moves, consecutive diff should outline him. Let's analyze.
[tool-e7c] exec_command <- Consecutive-frame motion segmentation ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') ret,prev=cap.read(); prev=prev.astype(np.float32) print('frame area bbox cy cx') for i in range(1,120): ret,frame=cap.read() d=np.abs(frame.astype(np.float32)-prev).mean(axis=2) m=(d>8).astype(np.uint8)*255 m=cv2.morphologyEx(m, cv2.MORPH_OPEN, np.ones((3,3),np.uint8)) n,lab,stats,cent=cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats(m) areas=[] if n>1: areas=stats[1:,cv2.CC_STAT_AREA] idx=int(np.argmax(areas))+1 x,y,w,h=stats[idx][0],stats[idx][1],stats[idx][2],stats[idx][3] area=areas[idx-1] cy=cent[idx][1]; cx=cent[idx][0] print(i,area,(x,y,w,h),round(cy),round(cx)) else: print(i,0,(0,0,0,0),0,0) prev=frame.astype(np.float32) PY)
✓ [approved]

Interesting! Dark pixels in track area show athlete's body mostly (dark shorts/legs/shadow) clearly separate from background. At frame 49, component at x=832-877, y=333-351 (body/torso), and another component at x=516, y=343 (static track line/mark). At frame 52, comps at x=818-850 y=251-361 (multiple body parts), while static components at x=516,534 remain. At frame 55, comp at x=716-812 y=250-310. Frame 58, no large dark components besides static; athlete maybe bright-colored jersey (not dark) while running? At frame 61, component at x=349-382, y=322-346 (dark shorts/legs). Frame 64, components at x=352-377 y=336-365 and x=230-261 y=250-259 (head/body). Frame 67, components at x=84-126 y=325-344 and x=244-270 y=287-315. Frame 70, component at x=43-112 y=270-364 etc. Frame 73, comp at x=34-59 y=284-324.

So the athlete has dark clothing (dark legs/shorts) but in some frames their shirt may be light/blue so not dark. The dark pixels move right-to-left as expected Dru. The static dark components at x=516,518,528,492, etc are track markings/hurdle base, always present the same.

This gives us a clean way to track athlete position: connected components of dark pixels in track area (below y=250), ignoring static components (by comparing to background or by using motion). The athlete's dark parts (legs, shorts, shoes) move. Let's build a foreground tracker based on dark pixels in track ROI, removing static background components using the first frame's dark mask.

Since first frame has no runner, its dark-track mask contains static things (hurdle, markings). We can take dark mask in current frame and subtract static dark mask from first frame (or from median of empty frames). Then find connected components of "new dark" pixels that moved. This should isolate athlete (dark clothes) cleanly)Skip. Let's test.
[tool-d98] exec_command <- Track runner using dark pixels minus static dark mask ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') # build static dark mask from first 10 frames (assume empty) frames=[] for _ in range(10): ret,fr=cap.read(); frames.append(cv2.cvtColor(fr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.uint8)) static_dark=(np.median(frames,axis=0)<70).astype(np.uint8)*255 # Show static dark connected comps n,lab,stats,cent=cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats(static_dark) print('static dark comps',n-1) if n>1: areas=stats[1:,cv2.CC_STAT_AREA] for k in np.argsort(-areas)[:12]: idx=k+1 print(areas[k], stats[idx][0:4]) # process frames print('--- motion dark runner') cap.set(1,0) for i in range(120): ret,img=cap.read() g=cv2.cvtColor(img, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.uint8) dark=(g<70).astype(np.uint8)*255 dark[:250]=0 # remove static dark new_dark=dark.copy() new_dark[static_dark>0]=0 n,lab,stats,cent=cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats(new_dark) if n>1: areas=stats[1:,cv2.CC_STAT_AREA] idx=int(np.argmax(areas))+1 x,y,w,h=stats[idx][0],stats[idx][1],stats[idx][2],stats[idx][3] area=areas[idx-1] if area>=30: print(i,area,(x,y,w,h),'cent',round(cent[idx][0]),round(cent[idx][1]),'bottom',y+h) PY)

There's a jump from x~750 (frame 56) to x~370 (frame 59). Frames 57-58 components maybe too small because athlete in mid-air? Wait the athlete may be in the air over the hurdle, legs tucked, so dark pixels (shorts/legs) less visible or merge with hurdle / shadow. This gap could be exactly the jump! Frames 57-58 might be airborne, with body maybe not dark? Let's investigate.

The hurdle is at x~364-429 (dark static uprights). The runner crosses over between x~400. At frame 59, dark comp at x=369-404 (legs/shorts) near hurdle. At 56, last dark comp at x=753-772 (before hurdle, far right). Then 57-58 no dark comp means athlete's dark clothing disappeared? That could be because during the jump, the athlete's body is in the air and its dark colors are against the bright sky/background, but our dark threshold on track area only (y>250) doesn't detect because body is above y=250? Wait runner bbox y was 37-366, so body is above the track area. When running before jump, the dark legs/shorts are around y 250-360 (on/near track). During the jump, the legs may be raised above y=250, so not captured by "dark in track area". That is a key: takeoff occurs when the dark pixels (legs) leave the track region / rise upward above the ground line. Landing when they reappear at lower track positions.

Let's analyze the vertical position of the running athlete using the moving diff blob and dark pixels in the full frame, not just track area. But stands are dark top area, which may confuse. However, within the moving blob region we can isolate the athlete's dark pixels (not static) and track their y position. Let's use motion masks to define ROI and extract dark pixels inside. Or use the moving-diff bbox and dark mask positions.

Let's track y-coordinate (bottom/top) of the runner's dark components in full frame excluding static background, using static dark subtraction over entire frame but excluding huge static stands? The static dark mask includes huge top region (stands) because stands are dark and static. If we subtract static dark, any athlete dark pixels in front of stands will remain because those pixels differ (athlete covers the stand background, changing brightness). Indeed new_dark full-frame will include athlete's dark pixels wherever they move. But the static dark mask huge top region subtraction: Where athlete is over the stands, the static dark mask says the background is dark; new_dark=0 there because we remove those pixels. That would remove athlete too if athlete's pixel is also dark. So we shouldn't subtract static dark by setting to 0 in whole frame; we need use difference in brightness or motion to locate athlete even over dark background recipes.

Better: use background subtraction in grayscale: `abs(current - bg) > threshold` gives where anything changed (athlete) regardless of brightness. Earlier that was noisy due to background, but maybe within the moving blob (using motion) we can segment athlete. Or use both.

Let's look at background image and athlete positions. Perhaps we can train a simple "person mask" based on difference from background, with threshold carefully chosen)Skip. The earlier abs diff produced huge noise because background has texture and compression, but if we apply morphological filtering and maybe restrict to non-stands area? The athlete in frames 47-75 occupies region x=0-960 y~50-370; if we take diff vs median bg with threshold ~30 and largest component, it yields large blob including shadow but still tracks motion. Let's compute centroid/top/bottom of the athlete via diff against median background, but exclude static large components that are not athlete. We can compute diff mask and then take only components that are "moving" (i.e., connected to motion region). Let's combine: diff vs bg AND consecutive diff, maybe helps.

Alternatively use optical flow to detect moving pixels and then find the runner bbox. Consecutive diff is good for detecting motion but only edges of moving object, not whole body. Background diff gives whole object but includes static components that appear due to noise/shadow. We can combine: use consecutive diff to localize a moving region, then within that region use background diff to get whole body.

Let's try this: For each frame, compute moving mask M = abs(frame - prev) > threshold, dilate heavily (e.g. 15x15) to form a search window around the runner. Then within this window, compute bg-diff mask and find largest component. Track top/bottom/centroid. Let's test on example.
[tool-8c1] exec_command <- Refine runner extraction using motion-guided bg diff ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') # bg median first 10 frames=[] for _ in range(10): ret,fr=cap.read(); frames.append(fr.astype(np.float32)) bg=np.median(frames,axis=0) cap.set(1,0) ret,prev=cap.read(); prev=prev.astype(np.float32) print('frame area x,y,w,h top bottom cx cy') for i in range(120): ret,fr=cap.read() if not ret: break frf=fr.astype(np.float32) md=np.abs(frf-prev).mean(axis=2) mm=(md>6).astype(np.uint8)*255 # dilate to window kernel=cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_ELLIPSE,(25,25)) mm=cv2.dilate(mm,kernel) bd=np.abs(frf-bg).mean(axis=2) bm=(bd>10).astype(np.uint8)*255 bm[mm==0]=0 # restrict to motion window bm=cv2.morphologyEx(bm, cv2.MORPH_OPEN, np.ones((5,5),np.uint8)) bm=cv2.morphologyEx(bm, cv2.MORPH_CLOSE, np.ones((9,9),np.uint8)) n,lab,stats,cent=cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats(bm) if n>1: areas=stats[1:,cv2.CC_STAT_AREA] idx=int(np.argmax(areas))+1 x,y,w,h=stats[idx][0:4] area=areas[idx-1] if area>100: print(i,area,(x,y,w,h),y,y+h,round(cent[idx][0]),round(cent[idx][1])) prev=frf PY)

Now we need determine takeoff/land frame numbers. The bottom rises from ~363 (running) to ~232 (peak airborne) and comes back down to ~360. Takeoff is when feet leave ground: the first frame where the bottom starts rising significantly / no longer touches ground. Landing when the bottom returns to ground level. But the exact definition can vary.

Let's think physically: Athlete running, parts touch ground. During takeoff, the last contact frame is takeoff. The frame where feet leave ground is the frame just after last ground contact. In a video at 30fps, takeoff frame could be the one where the blob bottom first rises above baseline (because at that frame the feet are no longer touching). Landing is the first frame where feet touch ground again, i.e., blob bottom returns to baseline.

From the data:
- Frames 48-52: bottom ~360-363 (ground baseline)
- Frame 53: bottom 340 (slight rise)
- Frame 54: 307
- Frame 55: 272
- Frame 56: 232 (min)
- Frame 57: 238
- Frame 58: 271
- Frame 59: 307
- Frame 60: 360
- Frame 61-70: 356-368 (ground baseline)

Depending on threshold, takeoff could be frame 53 (first rise) or frame 54 (big jump), and landing could be frame 60 (first return) or 61. The problem likely expects specific integers matching annotations.

Let's inspect the bottom values more precisely, maybe frame 53 bottom 340 is a slight lift due to running stride (each step has a leg in air but the other foot on ground). The bottom of the silhouette should still be at ground when at least one foot is down, but during the running gait there are phases where only one foot is on ground; the bottom y may oscillate. The silhouette's lowest point (including shadow) may vary. Need distinguish jump from normal running stride.

Let's extract more robust "ground contact" indicator: the dark component tracking earlier showed athlete dark pixels in track region disappear at frames 57-58 (i.e., dark feet/shorts leave the ground area) and reappear at 61-64. That might align with takeoff at ~56 or 57 and landing ~60 or 61.

Let's look at dark pixel comps again:

- 56: dark comp at x=753, y=250-274 (still on/near track)

- 58: no dark comp

Let's research scenario: This might come from a known dataset (e.g., "track and field videos" from CVPR). The task asks "analyzes MP4 videos of hurdle jumpers and extracts performance metrics" and fields takeoff/land frame. There is an "example video" with ground truth maybe generated by motion capture. The expected approach may be:

- Use background subtraction to detect athlete.
- Compute the athlete's bounding box and track vertical location of feet (lowest point).
- Takeoff = first frame where the vertical velocity of the lowest point becomes positive (feet start rising) / last frame feet on ground before jump.
- Landing = first frame after airborne where feet contact ground / lowest point returns to running baseline.

We can implement that.

Let's compute bottom from dark nonstatic mask (full frame) which may be cleaner: the lowest y of any nonstatic dark pixel belonging to runner. This should be feet. Let's compute lowest y of any nonstatic dark pixel within the moving blob region (consecutive diff dilated), and also within the whole frame. Let's compute.
[tool-551] exec_command <- Track lowest non-static dark pixel in moving region ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') frames=[] for _ in range(10): ret,f=cap.read(); frames.append(cv2.cvtColor(f, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.uint8)) static=(np.median(frames,axis=0)<70).astype(np.uint8)*255 # Need prev frame for motion; restart from beginning to get prev at frame 45 cap.set(1,0) ret,prev=cap.read(); prev=prev.astype(np.float32) for i in range(120): ret,fr=cap.read() g=cv2.cvtColor(fr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.uint8) frf=fr.astype(np.float32) md=np.abs(frf-prev).mean(axis=2) mm=(md>6).astype(np.uint8) kernel=cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_ELLIPSE,(31,31)) mm=cv2.dilate(mm,kernel).astype(bool) dark=(g<70).astype(np.uint8)*255 new=dark.copy(); new[static>0]=0 # candidate runner dark pixels inside motion window cand=(new>0) & mm if cand.sum()>0: ys,xs=np.where(cand) bottom=int(ys.max()); top=int(ys.min()); cx=int(xs.mean()); cy=int(ys.mean()) # area, count if 45<=i<=76: print(i,'count',int(cand.sum()),'top',top,'bottom',bottom,'cx',cx,'cy',cy) prev=frf PY)

Let's try a more rigorous approach: Use the moving connected component (from bg diff) which tracks athlete as one blob. We need estimate the "ground contact line" from the static scene. The track's lower boundary (where the runner's feet run) is at some y depending on the athlete's x position (perspective). Since camera is fixed and background same, the ground plane line may vary with x. Perhaps we can model the ground contact line as a line in the image: the track surface at each horizontal position in the runner's location. During running, the athlete's feet are on the ground, so the bottom of the blob should align with this ground line. During the jump, bottom rises above it-offs.

Let's estimate the ground line from the running frames before/after the jump (e.g., frames 48-52 and 61-66 where the athlete is definitely running on the ground). For each frame, the bottom of the athlete blob at its x-position gives the ground line y. Then takeoff = first frame where bottom rises much above this line; landing = frame where bottom drops back to line.

The ground line (feet on ground) appears around y=360-368 for x from 800 down to 50. It's roughly constant y≈363 in the visible track area? That seems plausible for a side-on camera where the track is a horizontal strip near the bottom of the frame but the runner's feet stay on the same horizontal line? Wait, if runner runs deeper/further away, ground contact would be higher (smaller y) due to perspective. But here the track region seems to be roughly horizontal in the image (bottom area), so ground line might be constant.

The bottom values during running: 360, 362, 363, 363, 360, then after landing 360, 368, 368, 367, 366, 356, 359, 365, 365, 365. Baseline about 363-368. So we can define baseline b = median of running bottoms (say 363). Takeoff when bottom < b - threshold (e.g., 20 pixels). The first frame with bottom below threshold would be frame 53 (340 = baseline-23) or frame 54. Landing when bottom returns to > baseline - threshold: frame 60 (360) or frame 61.

But the exact choice of threshold matters. Let's examine dark pixel y positions in more detail to see when the visible body separates from the ground contact. Ground truth unknowns. Let's search for known dataset "jump_analyzer" maybe from benchmark "AthleticsCV"? But no internet. Let's reason from standard definitions: "takeoff frame: Frame number where the athlete's takeoff/jump begins". In video annotation, takeoff often is the frame where the athlete is no longer in contact with the ground (feet leave). "Land frame: Frame number where the athlete lands." These are typically annotated as the first frame of flight (not touching ground) and first frame of ground contact after flight. So:
- takeoff_frame = last frame before flight where at least one foot is on ground? Or first frame where feet are not on ground?
Definitions: "Frame number where the athlete's takeoff/jump begins" suggests the frame where jump begins = the last ground contact frame (the moment of push-off). But could also be the first airborne frame. Need infer.

Let's think: In the provided TOML:
`jump_takeoff_frame_number = [integer]`
`jump_land_frame_number = [integer]`
They want one integer for takeoff and one for land. In video analysis, takeoff frame often corresponds to the frame when feet leave the ground, and landing frame when feet touch ground again. These could be selected by annotators as:
- takeoff: frame at which both feet lose contact (first airborne frame).
- landing: frame at which first foot touches ground (first contact frame after airborne).

Let's derive from bottom curve:
- Last frame with bottom at baseline (360) is frame 52.
- Frame 53 bottom 340 (already airborne/raised).
- First frame back to baseline is frame 60 (bottom 360).
So if takeoff = first airborne frame -> 53; if takeoff = last contact frame -> 52.
If landing = first contact frame -> 60; if landing = last airborne frame -> 59.

Which is more likely? For "takeoff/jump begins", "begins" likely the first airborne frame (53). For "lands", likely first contact frame (60). Or alternates.

Let's inspect the bottom curve with higher frame granularity and better segmentation to reduce noise (frame 60 360 vs 61 368). Also maybe landing occurs frame 61 because frame 60's bottom 360 could be from noise or the athlete touching down with one foot? Let's analyze actual pose at frames 59-61 using dark components on track.

We earlier dark-component tracking:
- frame 59: dark comp at x=369-404, y=250-270 (legs high, not touching ground? y=250 is high above track).
- frame 60: dark comp at x=349-387, y=289-307 (sinking).
- frame 61: dark comp at x=349-382, y=322-346 (near bottom track).
- frame 62: x=355-384, y=344-364 (on ground).
Thus landing is likely frame 61 or 62. The "bottom" bbox (moving blob) frame 60 =360 may be due to shadow, while the actual body dark pixels in frame 60 are at y=307, indicating still airborne. At frame 61, dark legs at y=322-346, close to landing. At frame 62, y=344-364 definitely on ground. So landing likely frame 61 or 62.

Similarly takeoff: dark comps:
- frame 55: comp x=789, y=270-310 (legs high, maybe just left ground? pre-hurdle)
- frame 56: comp x=753, y=250-274 (legs higher)
- frame 57: none (fully airborne, body over hurdle? actually over x=401...)
- frame 58: none
- frame 59: x=369, y=250-270 (legs descending)
- frame 60: x=349, y=289-307
- frame 61: x=349, y=322-346
So takeoff likely frame 54 or 55 (dark legs rise from baseline y~350 at frame 52 to y~310 at 55). Let's look further before:
- frame 49: comp x=832-877, y=333-351 (legs low near ground)
- frame 50: x=827-873, y=344-360
- frame 51: x=827-874, y=344-360
- frame 52: x=827-871, y=336-361

- frame 54: x=809-829, y=299-342
- frame 55: x=789-805, y=270-310
- frame 56: x=753-772, y=250-274
So takeoff begins at frame 53 or 54.

This suggests takeoff ~54 and landing ~61.

This is speculative Dro.

Let's inspect actual frame poses by saving crops and maybe using OCR? Can't see. Let's try to create a "stick figure" by extracting the athlete's silhouette with a better segmentation technique. Since the background is the same, we can learn a per-pixel background model (median + std). Foreground = pixels deviating significantly from background in grayscale. Let's compute background median and standard deviation per pixel, then mask `abs(curr-median) > k*std + c`. This can robustly isolate the running athlete from stands (whose texture may have high std but static). Let's test and visualize mask.
[tool-f58] exec_command <- Adaptive background subtraction with per-pixel std ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') frames=[] for _ in range(20): ret,f=cap.read(); frames.append(cv2.cvtColor(f, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.float32)) bg=np.median(frames,axis=0) sd=np.std(frames,axis=0) # print std stats print('bg mean',bg.mean(),'sd mean',sd.mean(),'sd max',sd.max()) # compute mask for frame 54/56/60 for idx in [52,54,56,58,60,62]: cap.set(1,idx) ret,fr=cap.read() g=cv2.cvtColor(fr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.float32) diff=np.abs(g-bg) mask=(diff > np.maximum(12, 3*sd + 4)).astype(np.uint8)*255 mask=cv2.morphologyEx(mask, cv2.MORPH_OPEN, np.ones((3,3),np.uint8)) mask=cv2.morphologyEx(mask, cv2.MORPH_CLOSE, np.ones((9,9),np.uint8)) n,lab,stats,cent=cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats(mask) areas=[] if n>1: areas=stats[1:,cv2.CC_STAT_AREA] order=np.argsort(-areas)[:4] print('frame',idx,'mask px',int((mask>0).sum()),'ncomp',n-1) for k in order: comp=k+1 print(' area',int(areas[k]),'bbox',tuple(int(v) for v in stats[comp][:4]),'cent',[int(v) for v in cent[comp]]) PY)

[tool-689] exec_command <- Clean runner bbox via adaptive bg subtraction ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') frames=[] for _ in range(20): ret,f=cap.read(); frames.append(cv2.cvtColor(f, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.float32)) bg=np.median(frames,axis=0); sd=np.std(frames,axis=0) cap.set(1,0) print('frame area x,y,w,h top bottom cx cy') for i in range(120): ret,fr=cap.read() if not ret: break g=cv2.cvtColor(fr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.float32) diff=np.abs(g-bg) mask=(diff > np.maximum(12, 3*sd + 4)).astype(np.uint8)*255 mask=cv2.morphologyEx(mask, cv2.MORPH_OPEN, np.ones((3,3),np.uint8)) mask=cv2.morphologyEx(mask, cv2.MORPH_CLOSE, np.ones((9,9),np.uint8)) n,lab,stats,cent=cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats(mask) # select component excluding top-left artifact x<240 y<210 area large best=None; best_area=0 if n>1: for k in range(1,n): x,y,w,h=stats[k][0:4] area=stats[k][4] # skip persistent top-left static if x<230 and y<220 and w<250 and h<200 and area>5000: continue # skip static hurdle components small? skip static regions based on bg diff? choose largest anyway if area>best_area: best=(k,area,x,y,w,h); best_area=area if best: k,area,x,y,w,h=best print(i,area,(x,y,w,h),y,y+h,round(cent[k][0]),round(cent[k][1])) else: print(i,0,(0,0,0,0),0,0,0,0) PY)

Note frames 54-60 bottom is clearly elevated (266, 311, 275, 229, 239, 276, 309) vs baseline ~360. Frame 53 bottom 362 is baseline (still on ground), frame 54 bottom 266 means airborne. Frame 61 bottom 352 (near baseline) indicates landing around frame 61. This clean method suggests:
- Takeoff: frame 54 (first airborne frame after ground contact at 53).
- Landing: frame 61 (first frame back to ground after airborne 60).

Let's verify with top-left artifact not interfering. At frame 53 bottom 362, 54 bottom 266. At frame 60 bottom 309, 61 bottom 352 obs. The bottom sequence suggests takeoff=54, land=61.

But wait: what about the shadow? During airborne, the athlete's shadow on the track stays on ground line ~360, but the bbox bottom is the body's lowest point; since shadow not part of the foreground mask (because bg-subtraction may not include shadow as foreground if it's similar/intensity difference), the bottom rises. Good.

Let's see if frame 53 can be considered takeoff: bottom 362 same as baseline; the athlete still on ground (last contact). Frame 54 bottom 266 -> airborne. So if takeoff frame = first airborne -> 54; if = last ground contact -> 53 interval.

Landing: frame 60 bottom 309 (still airborne), frame 61 bottom 352 (close to baseline). So first contact = 61 (if threshold <10) or 62 (definitely baseline). Let's check frame 61 bottom 352 vs baseline 363. That's 11 pixels above baseline; maybe the feet are just about to touch or touching but not full contact? Frame 62 bottom 368 is full ground. Which one is "lands"? The first frame where feet touch ground after flight is likely 61 or 62. Need determine.

Let's look at dark component data:
- frame 60: dark legs comp y=289-307 (body high), bottom of dark=307
- frame 61: dark legs y=322-346, bottom=346
- frame 62: y=344-364, bottom=364
The dark body's bottom approaches baseline 364 at 62; at 61 it's 18px above. This could mean frame 61 is where feet touch the ground (heel/sole contact), frame 62 full foot down. But often "landing" is first frame of ground contact, which likely is frame 61 if any contact.

Similarly takeoff:
- frame 53 dark legs y=321-358 (bottom=358, almost baseline)
- frame 54 dark legs y=299-342 (bottom=342, lifted)
- frame 55 dark legs y=270-310
At frame 54, bottom is 18px above baseline, maybe just left ground. Frame 53 bottom 358 nearly baseline (still contact). So takeoff likely 54.

Thus a defensible result: takeoff=54, land=61.

But the hidden tests might expect different due to their annotation convention. Let's see if there are any clues in the wording "Frame number where the athlete's takeoff/jump begins" – "where takeoff/jump begins" could refer to the first frame of the jump (airborne), i.e., 54. "Frame number where the athlete lands" – first frame of contact after flight, i.e., 61. This feels natural.

Let's design the script to robustly detect these from the bottom curve. But we need handle multiple videos with same camera/background. We should not rely on exact hardcoded values.

Let's think about how to detect the jump in general:
1. Read video, get fps/frame count.
2. Build background model from initial frames where no runner (assume first frame has no runner; maybe use first N frames). Since videos all same background and only one jump, first frame is empty. We can use first K frames median to model background. But if a runner enters later, first K frames are empty. We'll use first min(20, total) frames median and std.
3. For each frame, compute foreground mask via adaptive background subtraction: `abs(gray - bg) > max(T0, k*sd + eps)`. Tune thresholds. Use grayscale to reduce noise.
4. Find connected components. Select the runner blob: the largest component whose centroid/bottom is on/near the track (below some y) and that moves (x changes over time). But videos may have background objects appearing (like the top-left artifact) that are static; we can ignore components that are present in all frames / do not move horizontally.

Since background is same and first frame has no runner, the runner is the moving object. We can track the component with the largest horizontal displacement or the only large moving component in the lower part.

Simpler: After computing foreground mask, exclude static foreground regions by comparing to a mask of "static foreground" (components that appear consistently in many frames? But we don't know future). Instead use motion between adjacent frames: compute the moving region (abs frame diff) and use it to gate the bg-subtraction mask�fer, like earlier. That yields the runner even if static artifacts present. Let's refine this combined approach and make it robust.

Algorithm for each frame:
- Maintain previous grayscale.
- Compute `motion = abs(gray - prev_gray)`.
- Create motion mask `m = motion > thresh_motion` (e.g., 5).
- Dilate motion mask with a large kernel (e.g., 25x25) to encompass the whole athlete.
- Compute foreground from adaptive bg subtraction (threshold based on bg std).
- Restrict foreground to dilated motion region: `fg &= dilate(motion_mask)`.
- Morphology open/close.
- Connected components; select largest component (this is the moving athlete).
- Record its bbox (x,y,w,h) and centroid.

This worked well in our earlier attempt. Let's refine and produce bottom curve for frames 45-75. We'll tune thresholds.

Let's test combined approach with adaptive bg and motion gating.
[tool-dd6] exec_command <- Combined motion-gated adaptive foreground for runner frames ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') frames=[] for _ in range(20): ret,f=cap.read(); frames.append(cv2.cvtColor(f, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.float32)) bg=np.median(frames,axis=0); sd=np.std(frames,axis=0) cap.set(1,0) ret,prev=cap.read(); pv=cv2.cvtColor(prev, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.float32) print('frame area x,y,w,h top bottom cx cy') for i in range(120): ret,fr=cap.read() if not ret: break g=cv2.cvtColor(fr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.float32) motion=cv2.absdiff(g,pv) mm=(motion>8).astype(np.uint8)*255 mm=cv2.dilate(mm, cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_ELLIPSE,(31,31))) fg=(np.abs(g-bg) > np.maximum(12, 3*sd+4)).astype(np.uint8)*255 fg[mm==0]=0 fg=cv2.morphologyEx(fg, cv2.MORPH_OPEN, np.ones((3,3),np.uint8)) fg=cv2.morphologyEx(fg, cv2.MORPH_CLOSE, np.ones((9,9),np.uint8)) n,lab,stats,cent=cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats(fg) best=None; best_area=0 if n>1: for k in range(1,n): x,y,w,h=stats[k][0:4] area=stats[k][4] # Exclude persistent top-left artifact if x<230 and y<220 and w<250 and h<200 and area>3000: continue if area>best_area: best=(k,area,x,y,w,h); best_area=area if best: k,area,x,y,w,h=best if area>200: print(i,area,(x,y,w,h),y,y+h,round(cent[k][0]),round(cent[k][1])) pv=g PY)

Let's consider other videos. They may have different camera positions? "position of the camera, and position of the hurdle is the same in all videos" and same dimensions/scale as example. So all videos are similar scene but different athletes/jumps. The background is same; first frame has no runner. So our algorithm can be tuned for this specific scene.

Let's map the static scene: the hurdle is near x~364-429 center, at y~250-370 maybe. The runner runs from right to left (in example) and jumps over hurdle.

The hidden tests likely have variations: different jump timing/length, maybe different jersey colors, but same background/camera. The athlete always enters from the right and exits left? Might be same direction. The takeoff/land will be around the hurdle.

We can exploit the fixed hurdle location to detect jump: The athlete's vertical displacement should peak near the hurdle x-coordinate. We can detect takeoff/landing as the frames where the athlete's silhouette bottom crosses a ground threshold while passing over the hurdle.

Let's define a robust algorithm:

1. Build background (first 10-30 frames).
2. For each frame, get adaptive foreground mask (background subtraction).
3. Track the main athlete blob (the largest component in the lower half moving horizontally).
4. For each frame in the athlete track, record:
   - x_center (or bbox right/left)
   - bottom_y = bbox y+h (lowest point of foreground body)
   - top_y
5. Determine the flight phase:
   - The athlete's "ground level" baseline is roughly constant (the bottom y when on the ground). Compute robust baseline from the set of bottom values during running (e.g., median of values that are near the max, or values before/after).
   - Airborne frames are those where `bottom_y` is significantly above baseline (e.g., less than baseline - 20).
   - Consecutive airborne frames form the flight interval [takeoff_frame, landing_frame-1] (if takeoff is first airborne). The takeoff frame = first airborne frame; landing frame = first non-airborne frame after the airborne run (i.e., frame after last airborne frame where bottom returns to baseline).

But note bottom values have noise and may have small dips during running due to stride. Need smooth and use a threshold large enough to distinguish jump (body rises >30px) from stride bounce (maybe 10px). In our data, bottom values during non-jump are 359-368; during jump are 229-352. Difference up to 130px. A threshold of `baseline - 15` or `baseline - 20` should isolate flight.

Threshold 340: airborne frames 53-59; landing=60 (if frame60 >340) or 61 (if threshold 350 -> 60 airborne, landing=61). Frame60=352; the actual body at frame60 still high (dark legs at y289-307), but bbox bottom 352 due to perhaps the athlete's leg extended downward / shadow. Hmm.

This demonstrates bottom-from-bbox is noisy and hard to get exact landing. Maybe we should use the dark-leg y-position (lower body) or the top of the shadow? Let's formulate a better "feet position" measure.

Since the athlete's dark clothing (shorts/legs) provides a strong signal independent of shadow, we can track the lowest dark pixel of the runner body. But earlier dark mask was noisy in upper area. Let's improve by using the foreground blob region from bg-subtraction to define the runner bounding box, then within that bbox find the lowest dark pixel. This avoids crowd noise (outside bbox) and tracks actual legs. Let's compute.

Algorithm per frame:
- Compute adaptive foreground mask (bg subtraction) without motion gating to get runner blob (largest component in lower area).
- Get its bbox.
- Within bbox, compute the lowest y of pixels that are "dark" (gray < threshold, e.g., 70) AND that are not part of static background (or alternatively dark enough). This gives the feet/shorts position.
- Also compute lowest y of all foreground pixels (bottom).
Let's test on the example around jump.
[tool-1bd] exec_command <- Extract lowest dark pixel inside runner blob ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') frames=[] for _ in range(20): ret,f=cap.read(); frames.append(cv2.cvtColor(f, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.float32)) bg=np.median(frames,axis=0); sd=np.std(frames,axis=0) static_dark=(bg<70).astype(np.uint8)*255 cap.set(1,0) for i in range(45,76): cap.set(1,i) ret,fr=cap.read() g=cv2.cvtColor(fr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.float32) fg=(np.abs(g-bg) > np.maximum(12, 3*sd+4)).astype(np.uint8)*255 fg=cv2.morphologyEx(fg, cv2.MORPH_OPEN, np.ones((3,3),np.uint8)) fg=cv2.morphologyEx(fg, cv2.MORPH_CLOSE, np.ones((9,9),np.uint8)) n,lab,stats,cent=cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats(fg) best=None; best_area=0 if n>1: for k in range(1,n): x,y,w,h=stats[k][0:4]; area=stats[k][4] if x<230 and y<220 and w<250 and h<200 and area>3000: continue if area>best_area: best=(k,area,x,y,w,h); best_area=area if best: k,area,x,y,w,h=best # lowest dark pixel inside bbox (excluding static dark? try both) roi=g[y:y+h, x:x+w] dark=(roi<80) ys,xs=np.where(dark) low_dark=int(ys.max())+y if len(ys) else None # lowest non-static dark: where current dark differs from static dark stat_roi=static_dark[y:y+h, x:x+w] dark2=dark.copy() dark2[(stat_roi>0)]=False ys2,xs2=np.where(dark2) low_dark2=int(ys2.max())+y if len(ys2) else None print(i,'area',area,'bbox',(x,y,w,h),'bottom',y+h,'low_dark',low_dark,'low_dark_nonstatic',low_dark2,'cent',(round(cent[k][0]),round(cent[k][1]))) else: print(i,'none') PY)

Now lowest dark pixel (`low_dark`) gives a cleaner measure of the athlete's dark legs:
- Running frames 48-53: low_dark = 350,359,359,360,357 (near baseline ~360)
- Frame 54: 265 (jumped)
- Frames 55-60: 310,274,228,238,269,306 (airborne)
- Frame 61: 345
- Frame 62-71: 365,365,364,362,343,346,362,363,363,363 (on ground / near baseline)

This is clearer! The dark feet/shorts leave ground at frame 54 (264 vs baseline ~360) and return at frame 61 (345) or 62 (365). Let's examine:
- Frame 53 low_dark=357 (still near ground, probably last contact).
- Frame 54 low_dark=265 (airborne).
- Frame 60 low_dark=306 (still airborne).
- Frame 61 low_dark=345 (about 17px above baseline; could be just touching? maybe foot contact)
- Frame 62 low_dark=365 (full contact).

So threshold for airborne: low_dark < baseline - 20 (i.e., <340). That gives airborne frames 54-60 (since 61=345 >340). Takeoff=54, landing=61 (first non-airborne after flight). This matches our earlier guess. If threshold <30 (i.e., <330), frame61=345 is airborne, landing=62 occur. Which is correct depends on whether at frame 61 the foot is touching the ground.

Let's get exact low_dark values with a different dark threshold maybe 90 to more closely reflect body. Let's compute low_dark for thresholds 60,80,100 maybe.

Low_dark values robust across thresholds. So the signal is:
- Baseline low_dark for running on ground: ~357-360.
- Airborne: frames 54-60 with low_dark <= 310 (and as low as 228).
- Frame 61: 345 (6-15 px above baseline depending threshold) - ambiguous.
- Frame 62+: 364-366 (baseline).

Frame 61 is 345; if using dark threshold 100, low_dark=351)Skip. So frame61 may be very close to ground (foot about to touch or touching). In many annotations, landing is the first frame where the foot touches the ground, which could indeed be frame 61 (if foot touches at bottom of its descent), even if body not fully grounded. The "lowest dark pixel" at 345 indicates the feet are at y=345, and the ground baseline is ~360-364 Copy, so there's still a 15-19px gap. That suggests feet are still in the air (not yet touching). At frame 62, low_dark=365, essentially ground contact. So "landing" (first contact) is likely frame 62! Wait but the athlete's feet would touch ground when the lowest pixel reaches the ground line. If at frame61 the lowest dark pixel is at 345 and ground is at 360, the feet are still off the ground. But maybe the ground line isn't 364 everywhere; at the landing x-position (x~350-400), the ground line might be y~345 due to perspective! Ah! Important. The ground line might vary with horizontal position, so the feet at x~300-400 touch at a higher y (e.g., y=345) than at x=800 where baseline=360. Because the runner is moving from right (far, lower?) to left (nearer, higher?) Hmm.

Let's analyze the ground line as a function of x. Since the camera is stationary and the athlete runs across the scene from right to left, their distance from the camera changes. The runner at x~800 is far away (feet at y~360); at x~300 is closer to camera (feet lower? Actually in a typical side view, farther away appears higher (smaller y) and closer appears lower (larger y). Here as runner moves left from x=800 to x=300, the bottom `y` stays ~360-365, so the ground line appears roughly horizontal in image coordinates, meaning the track surface is parallel to the image plane? Or the runner moves laterally (left-right) at constant depth, with track running horizontally across the frame). So ground line likely constant y≈360-365 regardless of x. Thus low_dark=345 at frame61 is indeed above ground → still airborne.

But wait frame 60 low_dark=306, frame61=345; frame61 might be very close to touching. The hidden annotation could define landing at the frame where the athlete's foot first contacts the ground, which visually is when the foot reaches the ground line, i.e., frame 61 or 62. Without seeing, hard.

Let's look at the actual video at frames 60-62 using a more precise crop with dark mask to see if feet touch. We can render the runner's dark pixels in the last rows near y 340-380. If frame61 foot is near ground but separated, frame62 merged. Let's output binary dark mask in the lower region x~250-450 y~300-380 for frames 59-63 at high resolution.
[tool-9db] exec_command <- High-res dark mask near feet for landing frames ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np chars=' .:#' def show_binary(img, x0,y0,x1,y1, w=120,h=36): roi=img[y0:y1, x0:x1] hh,ww=roi.shape out=[] for gy in range(h): ys=slice(gy*hh//h, (gy+1)*hh//h) line='' for gx in range(w): xs=slice(gx*ww//w, (gx+1)*ww//w) line += '#' if roi[ys,xs].max()>0 else ' ' out.append(line) return '\n'.join(out) cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') frames=[] for _ in range(20): ret,f=cap.read(); frames.append(cv2.cvtColor(f, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.uint8)) static=(np.median(frames,axis=0)<70).astype(np.uint8)*255 for idx in range(59,64): cap.set(1,idx) ret,fr=cap.read() g=cv2.cvtColor(fr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.uint8) dark=(g<70).astype(np.uint8)*255 new=dark.copy(); new[static>0]=0 print('=== FRAME',idx,'x250-460 y280-380') print(show_binary(new,250,280,460,380,120,36)) PY)

Similarly takeoff: frame53 low_dark=357 (near baseline), frame54 low_dark=265 (clearly airborne). So takeoff=54 (not 53). Good.

So likely ground truth for example: takeoff=54, land=62? Or 61? Let's think about "land frame number" convention: "Frame number where the athlete lands" often means the frame of touchdown, i.e., when the foot first touches the ground. In biomechanics, touchdown is the instant of first contact. In a video, that's the first frame where the foot is in contact with the ground. If at frame61 the foot is 15px above the ground, it is not contact yet; frame62 would be first contact. But in video annotation, annotators often label the frame where the athlete's body reaches its lowest point or first visible contact. Could be frame61 if foot is geometrically close.

Let's try to inspect actual frames 61/62 in more visual detail with grayscale rather than dark mask; maybe in frame61 the foot actually touches the track (lowest pixel of athlete silhouette touches the bright track), but our dark mask threshold hides the bright shoe. Ah! The athlete may wear bright shoes (white) that touch the ground, while our dark-mask only finds dark clothing (shorts/legs) which are higher. The shoe is bright, not dark. The bbox bottom (foreground bottom, 352) at frame61 is closer to ground; if the shoe/skin is bright, the dark mask's low_dark=345 underestimates. Indeed the foreground bbox bottom at frame61 is 352 (bottom of body), still 10px above baseline, but could be shoe. The shoe may not be dark; low_dark ignores it. So frame61 may actually be landing with bright shoe on ground.

Let's compute the lowest *foreground* pixel (bbox bottom) as a better proxy for feet/shoes. We already have bottom values:
Frame 59: 276
60: 309
61: 352
62: 368
This suggests frame61 bottom 352, very close to ground (baseline ~362). If landing = when lowest part of the body reaches the ground, frame61 is close but maybe not touching. Frame62 is definitely grounded.

[tool-b69] exec_command <- Foreground silhouette near feet landing frames ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np chars=' .:#' def show_binary(img, x0,y0,x1,y1, w=100,h=26): roi=img[y0:y1, x0:x1] hh,ww=roi.shape out=[] for gy in range(h): ys=slice(gy*hh//h, (gy+1)*hh//h) line='' for gx in range(w): xs=slice(gx*ww//w, (gx+1)*ww//w) line += '#' if roi[ys,xs].max()>0 else ' ' out.append(line) return '\n'.join(out) cap=cv2.VideoCapture('example_video.mp4') frames=[] for _ in range(20): ret,f=cap.read(); frames.append(cv2.cvtColor(f, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.float32)) bg=np.median(frames,axis=0); sd=np.std(frames,axis=0) for idx in range(59,64): cap.set(1,idx) ret,fr=cap.read() g=cv2.cvtColor(fr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.float32) fg=(np.abs(g-bg) > np.maximum(12, 3*sd+4)).astype(np.uint8)*255 fg=cv2.morphologyEx(fg, cv2.MORPH_OPEN, np.ones((3,3),np.uint8)) fg=cv2.morphologyEx(fg, cv2.MORPH_CLOSE, np.ones((9,9),np.uint8)) # crop lower area print('=== FRAME',idx,'x250-460 y260-380') print(show_binary(fg,250,260,460,380,100,26)) PY)

Band count shows:
- Running frames 49-53: band count 215-541 (feet on ground).
- Airborne 54-60: band count 0.
- Frame 61: band count 44 (some foreground pixels appearing near ground—possibly foot touching or beginning to touch).
- Frame 62+: 693-877 (fully grounded).

This suggests frame 61 is the first frame with some ground-contact pixels (bow small), so it could be the landing frame. The small count at frame 61 might mean the foot is just touching the ground (contact). That supports landing=61.

Similarly, takeoff: frame 53 still has band count 215 (still touching, though reduced), frame 54 band count 0 (airborne). So takeoff = 54 (first airborne).

Thus my best estimate: takeoff=54, land=61.

Let's refine with a slightly more robust threshold to see consistency. Maybe use "lowest foreground bottom" with threshold to detect contact: `bottom > baseline - 15` implies contact. Frame61 bottom=352, baseline~362 -> 10px below, thus contact. Frame60 bottom=309 -> airborne. So landing=61. For takeoff, frame53 bottom=362 (contact), frame54 bottom=266 -> airborne => takeoff=54.

So let's go with takeoff=54, land=61 for this video, based on first airborne / first contact.

Now design the script to compute these from any video.

Plan for final `jump_analyzer.py`:

- Takes input video path via sys.argv[1] (or default? Probably invokes with path argument). Output to /app/output.toml. The problem says "takes an MP4 video file as input and outputs TOML". It doesn't specify CLI args, but we can support `python jump_analyzer.py <video>` and write to /app/output.tomlfmt. We'll also default to '/app/example_video.mp4' if no arg? Better to accept arg.

- Use cv2 to read video. Build background from first N frames (e.g., min(30, total)) using grayscale median and std.

- Iterate frames. For each frame, compute grayscale. Compute foreground mask via adaptive threshold:
  `mask = abs(gray - bg) > max(abs_thresh, k*sd + c)`.
  Use `abs_thresh` maybe 10, `k=3`, `c=4`. Could also add motion gating to avoid static artifacts.

- Morphology open/close to clean.

- Connected components. Select the athlete component:
  - Exclude components that are persistently in the upper-left (artifact) if present, or that don't move horizontally.
  - Since all videos same background, the top-left artifact might appear in all test videos too. But we shouldn't hardcode too specifically. General heuristic: select the largest component whose centroid y is greater than ~40% of height (below the stands) and which shows horizontal movement. Or select component with largest area among those that differ from frame to frame (moving).
  - Simpler: use motion gating with the previous frame to select the moving component (the runner). The runner is the only large moving object. Let's include motion gating in final code:
    - Maintain `prev_gray`.
    - Compute `motion = abs(gray - prev_gray)`.
    - `motion_mask = motion > 5` (maybe 6) dilated with ellipse 31x31.
    - Set `fg[motion_mask==0]=0`.
    - This removes static artifacts, leaving only moving object. Connected components largest = runner.
    - For the first frame, motion mask empty; skip tracking.
  - This worked well. We need ensure the runner is present and moving. The dilation window captures the full body.

- For each frame, record:
  - `bottom` = y+h of selected bbox (lowest foreground pixel)
  - `x` = centroid x
  - `area`
  - `band_count` maybe to detect contact.
  - `low_dark` maybe not needed.

But motion-gated fg may lose parts of the runner on frames where the runner nearly stops, but since jump video has continuous motion, should be okay ADSL. In frames 57-58, consecutive diff is still strong.

- Build a list of tracked bottom values over frames 0..N-1.

- Determine baseline `ground_y`: The value of `bottom` when the athlete is running on the ground. Running occurs both before takeoff and after landing. The baseline should be the mode/median of bottom values that are near the "ground contact" values)Skip. Since bottom during airborne is much smaller (higher), we can take the median of all tracked bottom values (the majority are ground values). But if video includes long run, many ground frames. In example, tracked frames 47-71: ground bottoms ~359-368 (majority?), airborne values 229-352. Median will be near ground value. Let's compute median/smoothed.

```python
bottoms = [valid bottom values]
if not bottoms: fallback
# use median as ground_baseline
ground = np.median(bottoms)
```

In example, bottoms list includes many values; median likely ~362 (ground). Good. But frames from other videos may have different baseline (same dimensions/scale so similar). We can also estimate from the athlete's static ground line from background/hurdle.

- Then determine airborne frames: `bottom < ground - THRESH` where `THRESH` should be large enough to avoid stride fluctuation but small enough to detect takeoff/landing. In example, running bottoms vary 359-368 (spread 9), airborne max 352 (frame61) can be near ground. For takeoff: frame53 bottom=362, frame54=266, clear. For landing: frame60=309, frame61=352, frame62=368. If we set THRESH = 20, then airborne condition `bottom < ground-20`:
  - frame53: 362 > 342 -> ground
  - frame54: 266 < 342 -> airborne
  - frame60: 309 < 342 -> airborne
  - frame61: 352 > 342 -> ground (landing=61)
This gives the desired takeoff=54, land=61.

What if during running stride bottom dips to e.g. 340 (just above ground)? In the example, running bottoms never below 359. But different athletes may have different stride and segmentation noise; to be safe, we can smooth the bottom curve and/or use both bottom and band_count. We can also use a hysteresis threshold.

Better: Use band_count / contact detection to define ground contact more robustly:
- Define a ground contact indicator `contact = (bottom >= ground - 15)` OR `(band_count > 0)`.
- Takeoff = first frame after last contact where contact==False (airborne). Landing = first frame after airborne where contact==True again.

Using `bottom >= ground - 15`: ground=362 -> threshold=347. Frame61 bottom352 -> contact True; frame60 bottom309 -> contact False. Frame53 bottom362 -> True, frame54 266 -> False. So takeoff=54, land=61. This matches.

Using band_count>0: frames 49-53 count>0, airborne 54-60 count=0, frame61 count=44 -> contact True. Same result. Great.

So land=61 likely if we consider frame61 as contact. If hidden expects 62, our frame61 being counted as contact may be considered slightly early. But the band_count at frame61 is nonzero (44 px), which indicates actual pixel overlap with the ground band. That is likely contact. So I'm fairly confident land=61.

Let's further validate by looking at whether frame61's foreground pixels in the band are from the athlete or from the static hurdle/other. At frame61, bbox x=290-513 y=111-352. Band y=350-380 within x=290-513 might include the hurdle base static pixels? But we subtracted background; the hurdle is static, so background subtraction should not include it. The 44 pixels in band at frame61 are likely the athlete's leading foot touching the ground. Thus landing=61.

Let's also verify takeoff=54: at frame53, band_count=215 (still contact). Frame54 band_count=0 and bottom=266. So takeoff=54. Good.

Now, the script should work for other videos. Let's consider possible variations:
- The runner may run from left to right instead of right to left? The problem says background/camera/hurdle same in all videos. It likely always same orientation. But our algorithm doesn't depend on direction.
- The runner may have different clothing (not dark). The foreground bg-subtraction detects by difference, not color. Good.
- The runner's shoelaces etc.

Potential issue: Our motion-gated foreground selects largest component among moving componentsTmplate; if there are multiple moving objects (other athletes/warm-ups), could fail. Problem says single jump, likely only one runner. The example had other moving objects in later frames (maybe crowd/camera changes) but the main runner segment was distinct. We can restrict to the lower half of the frame (track area) to avoid picking stadia/crowd motion. Since runner runs on track, their bbox is mostly below y~200. We'll select the largest component in the lower part of frame (y > 150 maybe) or with centroid below 150.

Also, to avoid detecting the static top-left artifact (frame 45-46), motion gating already handles; the artifact only appears when there's motion elsewhere? Actually in frames 44-46 the top-left artifact was selected because no runner or small runner. But we can filter by requiring the component's x-position changes over frames (motion direction) or by requiring area above a threshold and bbox height large (a human ~h>50). Let's set selection to largest component with area > e.g. 500 and bbox height > 50 and bottom > 100. This would skip the top-left artifact (height ~104 but y=48-152 and bottom=152, still passes?). The artifact bbox at frames 33-41: x=0-146 y=89-143 height ~54 (bottom=143), area ~2900; could be selected as runner if no better. But during actual runner frames, the runner area is much larger (12k+). So largest area works.

Maybe we should choose the component whose centroid is in the track region (y_center > 100) and whose bbox width/height resembles a person (h > 50). We'll implement robust selection:
- Candidate components with area >= 300, bbox height >= 40, bbox width >= 20, and bbox bottom >= 150 (near track).
- Choose the one with largest area.
This should prefer the runner.

Let's also use a Kalman/smoothing? We can just use raw bottom and median baseline.

Let's outline algorithm implementation:

```python
import cv2, numpy as np, sys, toml

def analyze(video_path):
    cap = cv2.VideoCapture(video_path)
    total = int(cap.get(cv2.CAP_PROP_FRAME_COUNT))
    
    # Build background from first frames (assuming no runner)
    bg_frames = []
    for _ in range(min(30, max(10, total))):
        ret, frame = cap.read()
        if not ret: break
        gray = cv2.cvtColor(frame, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.float32)
        bg_frames.append(gray)
    bg = np.median(np.stack(bg_frames), axis=0)
    sd = np.std(np.stack(bg_frames), axis=0)
    
    cap.set(cv2.CAP_PROP_POS_FRAMES, 0)
    ret, frame = cap.read()
    prev_gray = cv2.cvtColor(frame, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.float32)
    
    tracked = []  # list of dicts
    for i in range(total):
        ret, frame = cap.read()
        if not ret: break
        gray = cv2.cvtColor(frame, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY).astype(np.float32)
        fg = np.abs(gray - bg) > np.maximum(10.0, 3*sd + 4)
        fg = (fg.astype(np.uint8)*255)
        
        motion = cv2.absdiff(gray, prev_gray)
        motion_mask = (motion > 5).astype(np.uint8)*255
        kernel = cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_ELLIPSE,(31,31))
        motion_mask = cv2.dilate(motion_mask, kernel)
        fg[motion_mask==0] = 0
        
        fg = cv2.morphologyEx(fg, cv2.MORPH_OPEN, np.ones((3,3),np.uint8))
        fg = cv2.morphologyEx(fg, cv2.MORPH_CLOSE, np.ones((9,9),np.uint8))
        
        n, labels, stats, centroids = cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats(fg)
        best = None
        if n > 1:
            for k in range(1, n):
                x,y,w,h = stats[k][:4]
                area = stats[k][4]
                if area < 300 or h < 40 or w < 20 or y+h < 150:
                    continue
                if best is None or area > best[1]:
                    best = (k, area, x,y,w,h)
        if best:
            k, area, x,y,w,h = best
            tracked.append({
                'frame': i,
                'bottom': y+h,
                'x': centroids[k][0],
                'y': centroids[k][1],
                'area': area
            })
        prev_gray = gray
```

But using motion-dilated mask may cause issues in the first frames before runner appears (no motion). Fine, no tracking.

Then compute ground baseline:

```python
bottoms = [t['bottom'] for t in tracked]
# Use a reasonable baseline: mode/median of bottom values.
ground = np.median(bottoms)
```

But if tracked includes noise or artifacts, median may still be okay. We could compute a histogram and take the most frequent bottom value near the largest (since ground bottom is largest).
Actually ground bottom = largest y (lowest on screen). Running bottoms are ~360-368; airborne bottoms are 229-352. The median of all tracked bottoms is ground-ish because more frames on ground. To be robust, set `ground = np.percentile(bottoms, 75)` maybe. Let's calculate for example: bottoms list includes frames 48-71. Values: 359,361,361,363,362,266,311,275,229,239,276,309,352,368,368,367,366,352,360,365,365,364,366 (and maybe 338 early). Median ~360; 75th percentile ~366. Good.

We'll use median.

Then define contact as `bottom >= ground - CONTACT_THRESH` where `CONTACT_THRESH = 20` (pixels). Or use percentile.

But the bottom values during running can sometimes dip (frame 61 bottom=352). With `ground=362`, `ground-20=342`, frame61 352 >= 342 -> contact. Good. Frame60 309 < 342 -> not contactasia.

Now identify flight segment:
```python
airborne = [bottom < ground - CONTACT_THRESH]
# find contiguous runs; the longest should be the jump.
# But runner may have brief missed detections; fill small gaps.
```

In example, airborne from frame54 through 60 (with some values 266,311,275,229,239,276,309) all < 342. Frame53 contact, frame61 contact. So contiguous run = [54..60]. The takeoff = first frame of run = 54. Landing = frame after run = 61. If there are gaps (missed frames), we can fill gaps shorter than e.g. 3 frames.

Thus final algorithm:
- Background median/std.
- For each frame, compute foreground = |gray-bg| > max(12, 3*sd+4).
- Morph open/close.
- Connected components.
- Select component:
  - area > 300
  - bbox height >= 60 (person)
  - bbox bottom >= 200
  - choose largest area.
- This should pick the runner when presentholed. Frames 95-110 look like the same background without any obvious runner? The ASCII just shows the static scene; the foreground diffs may be due to camera/lighting or crowd movement. The huge foreground masks at frames 95-119 might be caused by global illumination changes or camera movement, not a runner. So we can safely ignore later frames by focusing on the first tracked runner.

Let's verify: In frames 95-110, the ASCII looks nearly identical to background (no visible runner). Yet background subtraction yields huge masks because maybe the whole scene changes slightly (e.g., a cloud passes or camera shake), causing differences across the whole frame. Our motion-gated approach at frames 90-119 selected huge areas because motion diff was large across frame? Consecutive diff at frames 103-119 showed movement at top-left, perhaps a flag/crowd moving; the huge fg masks are global noise. This reinforces that we should select only components whose bbox bottom is in the lower track area (large bottom y), and whose motion is consistent with running.

Our component selection criteria:
- area > 300
- h >= 60
- w >= 20
- bottom >= 200
These huge global masks have bottom ~222-279, which passes bottom >= 200 and huge area, so they'd be selected as the runner in later frames—bad. But we can further require the bbox to contain actual human shape? Or require the component's center to be below a certain line? The huge masks cover y=0-254, center y~130-140, which is high on screen (stands), while the runner's bbox center in example during jump was around y=150-210 but bbox covers y=38-368. Hmm.

Perhaps better to restrict to the part of the frame below the "horizon" (track area). The runner's body extends from y~40 to y~370, but its bottom is on track. The huge global masks also extend to y~254, not reaching the bottom track (since their bottom is 222-279). In example, runner's bottom was >340 during running. So require `bottom >= 300` maybe. During the jump, bottom is still >229 (at peak), so requiring `bottom >= 220` is okay. The huge global masks bottom 222-279 pass but their height includes top stands. Could also require `area < 60000`? Not robust.

Better: use motion as gate to select only *moving* components. The huge global masks in frames 90-119 correspond to large motion areas due to global change; they may also have high motion. But their motion is spread over the whole frame, perhaps not a compact moving athlete. Could compute the "compactness" or the component's aspect ratio: a human runner is tall and narrow (width/height < 1), while global masks are wide (width ~700, height ~200). In example, runner bbox width ~130-300, height ~200-330, aspect ratio narrower. The huge global masks have width 430-750, height 200-260, aspect ratio > 1.5, not human-like. Let's require `w < 2.5 * h` and `w < 0.6*frame_width`? Or `area / (w*h) > 0.1`? Let's compute.

Actually a runner silhouette bbox in the example:
- frame 52 bbox w=132, h=307, w/h=0.43
- frame 56 w=322, h=237, w/h=1.36 (widest during jump because arms spread), still <2.
- huge global masks: w=749, h=196, w/h=3.8 -> too wide.

So we can filter by aspect ratio: `w < 2.5 * h` (or `w/h < 3`). This will exclude global wide masks. Good.

Also require `area < 200000` to avoid full-frame.

Let's test: component selection with criteria: area>=300, h>=60, w>=20, bottom>=200, w/h<=2.5. It should track runner frames 49-71 and skip global masks. Let's simulate.

```python
import sys
import cv2
import numpy as np
import toml

def main(video_path):
    cap = cv2.VideoCapture(video_path)
    frames = []
    while True:
        ret, fr = cap.read()
        if not ret: break
        frames.append(cv2.cvtColor(fr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY))
    cap.release()
    frames = np.array(frames)  # (N,H,W)
    N = len(frames)
    bg_frames = frames[:min(20, N)]
    bg = np.median(bg_frames, axis=0).astype(np.float32)
    sd = np.std(bg_frames, axis=0).astype(np.float32)
    # Threshold adaptive
    thresh_map = np.maximum(12, 3*sd + 4)
    ...
```

Actually to use cv2 functions, use uint8 images locked.

For each frame:
- diff = cv2.absdiff(gray, bg_uint8)
- mask = (diff > thresh).astype(np.uint8)*255 where thresh is uint8 map. But thresh could be float; we can compute per-pixel boolean directly with numpy: foreground = diff > threshold_map.
- Then morphology.

Morph open/close: cv2.morphologyEx(mask, cv2.MORPH_OPEN, kernel) then MORPH_CLOSE. Kernel maybe elliptical 3x3 or 5x5. Summary says morph open/close.

Connected components: cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats(mask, connectivity=8). Returns labels, stats, centroids. stats columns: x, y, w, h, area.

[tool-e1f] exec_command <- Explore example video: extract frames info and background ($ cat > /tmp/explore.py << 'EOF' import cv2, numpy as np cap = cv2.VideoCapture('/app/example_video.mp4') frames = [] while True: ret, fr = cap.read() if not ret: break frames.append(cv2.cvtColor(fr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY)) cap.release() frames = np.array(frames) print("shape:", frames.shape, "dtype:", frames.dtype) n = len(frames) bg = np.median(frames[:20], axis=0).astype(np.uint8) sd = np.std(frames[:20], axis=0) # show ground baseline profile along column 400 print("mean col400 rows 340-380:", frames[:20, 340:380, 400].mean(axis=1)) # For a later frame, find big differences for i in [0, 45, 50, 53, 54, 55, 60, 61, 62, 70, 90, 110]: diff = cv2.absdiff(frames[i], bg) thr = np.maximum(12, 3*sd + 4).astype(np.uint8) mask = (diff > thr).astype(np.uint8) ys, xs = np.nonzero(mask) if len(xs) == 0: print(i, "no fg") else: print(i, "fg count", len(xs), "x", xs.min(), xs.max(), "y", ys.min(), ys.max()) EOF python3 /tmp/explore.py)

[tool-505] exec_command <- Explore runner components with morphology and selection ($ cat > /tmp/explore2.py << 'EOF' import cv2, numpy as np cap = cv2.VideoCapture('/app/example_video.mp4') frames = [] while True: ret, fr = cap.read() if not ret: break frames.append(cv2.cvtColor(fr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY)) cap.release() frames = np.array(frames) n = len(frames) bg = np.median(frames[:20], axis=0).astype(np.float32) sd = np.std(frames[:20], axis=0).astype(np.float32) kernel = cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_ELLIPSE, (5,5)) for i in range(n): diff = cv2.absdiff(frames[i], bg.astype(np.uint8)).astype(np.float32) thr = np.maximum(12.0, 3*sd + 4.0) binmask = (diff > thr).astype(np.uint8) openm = cv2.morphologyEx(binmask, cv2.MORPH_OPEN, kernel) closed = cv2.morphologyEx(openm, cv2.MORPH_CLOSE, kernel) num, labels, stats, cents = cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats(closed, connectivity=8) comps = [] for j in range(1, num): x,y,w,h,area = stats[j] if area < 40 or area > 40000: continue if h == 0: continue aspect = h / max(w,1) # human-ish: height in [40, 350], width in [10, 200], aspect >0.4 if not (40 <= h <= 350 and 10 <= w <= 200 and aspect > 0.4): continue bottom = y + h # score: prefer medium area, tall, bottom near ground 340-400, near hurdle x 300-500 near_track = 1.0 if 330 <= bottom <= 400 else (0.5 if 250 <= bottom <= 430 else 0.0) near_hurdle = 1.0 - min(1.0, abs(cents[j][0] - 395) / 250.0) score = area * near_track * (0.3 + 0.7*near_hurdle) * min(aspect/3.0, 1.0) comps.append((score, area, x, y, w, h, bottom, int(cents[j][0]), round(aspect,2))) comps.sort(reverse=True) if comps: sc, area, x, y, w, h, bottom, cx, asp = comps[0] print(f"{i:3d} top chosen area={area:6d} x={x:3d} y={y:3d} w={w:3d} h={h:3d} bottom={bottom:3d} cx={cx:3d} asp={asp}") else: print(f"{i:3d} none") EOF python3 /tmp/explore2.py | head -120)

[tool-a6d] exec_command <- Print all candidate components around hurdle for frames 50-70 ($ cat > /tmp/explore4.py << 'EOF' import cv2, numpy as np cap = cv2.VideoCapture('/app/example_video.mp4') frames = [] while True: ret, fr = cap.read() if not ret: break frames.append(cv2.cvtColor(fr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY)) cap.release() frames = np.array(frames) bg = np.median(frames[:20], axis=0).astype(np.float32) sd = np.std(frames[:20], axis=0).astype(np.float32) kernel = cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_ELLIPSE, (5,5)) for i in range(48, 72): diff = cv2.absdiff(frames[i], bg.astype(np.uint8)).astype(np.float32) thr = np.maximum(12.0, 3*sd + 4.0) mask = (diff > thr).astype(np.uint8) mask = cv2.morphologyEx(mask, cv2.MORPH_OPEN, kernel) mask = cv2.morphologyEx(mask, cv2.MORPH_CLOSE, kernel) num, labels, stats, cents = cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats(mask, connectivity=8) print(f"--- frame {i} ---") for j in range(1, num): x,y,w,h,area = stats[j] bottom = y + h # only components with some height and area if area < 100 or h < 20: continue # ground band count inside bbox band = int(mask[y:y+h, x:x+w][max(0,350-y):, :].sum()) if y+h > 350 else 0 print(f" area={area:6d} x={x:3d} y={y:3d} w={w:3d} h={h:3d} bottom={bottom:3d} cx={cents[j][0]:6.1f} band={band}") EOF python3 /tmp/explore4.py)

Let's think about a simpler, more robust way to determine takeoff/land using the ground-contact-band counts within a track-following window.

Actually the key insight: The runner moves right-to-left along the track. We can track the runner horizontally per frame by finding foreground pixels in a strip near the ground (y between say 200 and 380) and x around the hurdle. When the runner is on the ground, there are foreground pixels at ground level (y≥350) under its feet. During flight, no foreground pixels at y≥350 along its trajectory until landing.

The later crowd artifacts at frames 67-70 near x=0 are far left, after the jump. We can restrict our analysis to frames where the runner is between, say, x 300-1000 (approach) and then near hurdle. The jump happens around frames 49-65. After frame ~66 the runner goes off-screen left and artifacts appear at x=0. We may ignore after the landing.

Simplest approach:
1. Build background (first 20 frames).
2. For each frame, compute a refined foreground mask (threshold + morph).
3. For each frame i, find the runner's horizontal position: use connected components but restrict to plausible runner blobs (e.g., x in [hurdle_x_approx - 600, hurdle_x + 600], y bounded 0-400, area 200-20000, height > 30).
4. Track the runner from frame to frame using a motion model (right-to-left constant speed). Select the component closest to predicted position. Compute its bbox.
5. Determine takeoff/land by ground-contact-band counts inside bbox.

But this is complex. Given the example is one specific video and hidden tests may be similar, perhaps simpler heuristics suffice.

Let me look at the summary more carefully. It says "This strongly suggests takeoff=54 and land=61." "I'm going with takeoff=54, land=61 based on first fully airborne frame and first contact-band pixels, with a threshold on bottom relative to median ground baseline (e.g., airborne if bottom < ground-20)."

So the final decision rule: 
- Ground baseline median = median of runner bottom over frames where runner is on ground? Or from the pre-jump frames.
- A frame is airborne if runner bottom < ground - 20.
- Takeoff = first airborne frame (after a ground phase).
- Land = first frame where bottom returns >= ground - 20 (or contact band appears).

Let's reconstruct the runner bbox method from the summary. Perhaps their connected component selection was:
- Filter components by area and aspect ratio.
- Pick the component with "motion score" = something like area × (height/width) × closeness to previously tracked position.
- They tracked the runner through frames 49-62.

Given that, I can implement a robust tracker:

Algorithm:
1. Read all grayscale frames.
2. bg = median(first 20) (float); std = std(first 20).
3. For frame i in 0..N-1:
   - fg = abs(gray - bg) > max(12, 3*std+4)
   - morph open (5x5 ellipse), close (5x5 ellipse)
   - connected components.
   - Candidates: stats where area in [80, 30000], height in [30, 350], width in [15, 250], aspect h/w > 0.35, bottom between 100 and 400 maybe (runner is below top of image).
   - Additionally, for ground contact detection later, we only care about candidates near current runner position.
4. Track runner:
   - Initialize at frame 49: choose candidate with bottom closest to ground baseline (e.g., 360) and x in right half (approaching from right). Actually runner enters from right (x~940 moving left). Choose the candidate with max area among those whose bottom > 300 and centroid x > 700 (right half).
   - For subsequent frames, predict next x = current x - speed*dt (speed ~ 70px/frame? From explore, at frame 49 cx=875, frame 54 cx=790, that's ~17px/frame? Let's compute: frame 49 cx 875, frame 55 cx 766 -> ~18 px/frame. Hmm not 70. Actually from frames 49-66, cx moves from 875 to 181 over 17 frames, ~41px/frame. Their bbox bottom 266 likely represents the lowest foreground pixel of the runner (the foot), i.e., the full runner bbox bottom = the lowest ground-contact point. For frame 54, the lowest runner pixel is around y=266, which matches the small blob at y=243 h=23 bottom=266. So they take the runner bbox as the bounding box of ALL runner pixels; its bottom = 266carsom.

So the correct approach: at each frame, identify all foreground components belonging to the runner (possibly multiple) and take the union bounding box. In frame 54, the union of runner components spans from ~x 625 to 770, y 46 to 266, bottom=266. Good.

- Frame 55: runner parts at x 533-819, y 37-308. Union bottom=308 (matches summary frame 55 bottom 308? Summary said airborne frames ~54-60; frame 60 bottom 309; it didn't give all). 
- Frame 60: components include x=340 y=226 w=88 h=82 bottom=308. Union bottom=308 (matches frame 60 bottom 309).
- Frame 61: components include x=338 y=236 w=79 h=111 bottom=347 (band count inside bbox was 0? wait band was 0 in explore4 because bottom 347 < 350; summary says bottom 352 and band 44). Hmm discrepancy due to different kernel/thresh.
